# EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

**The Fundamental Problem — The Failure of Continuous Models**

Conventional economic models (DSGE, macroeconometric forecasts) have failed to anticipate major crises: 1929, 1973, 2008, 2022. Their error is structural: they treat the economy as a continuous space of adjustable variables, when in fact it functions as a discrete information system, with clear rupture thresholds and stable regimes (see Appendices Q and R on the aporia of established economic theories).

The structural crisis China is currently experiencing — the collapse of the property market, the suffocating debt of local governments, the halt to mega-infrastructure projects — is neither an anomaly nor an ideological failure. It is the predictable halt of a growth engine that has simply run out of steam.

**The Economic Heavenly Way — The 64→20 Invariant as a Universal Law**

We have established that the economy obeys a universal invariant, $64 \rightarrow 20$, which also manifests in particle physics (Rowlands), the genetic code, and complex systems in general (De Dominicis).

- 64 possible economic configurations are defined by six binary variables: Inflation, Purchasing Power, Profits, Rent Extraction, Dispersion, Land Regime.
- These 64 states are reduced to 20 stable attractors through a topological filtration (Merkabah geometry, Clifford algebra).
- Transitions between these 20 attractors are constrained by tropical belts and polar thresholds. Any direct transition without passing through a threshold triggers a retropolarity (systemic collapse).

This formalism is the mathematisation of correlative Chinese thought — the Wuxing and the Yi Jing — which has structured all ontologies through a network of correspondences for over two millennia. The Economic Heavenly Way (Tiandao Jingji) is the mathematised formulation of this structural wisdom applied to the regulation of economic systems.

**The Hidden Mechanism of Growth — Disequilibrium as an Engine**

Contrary to classical theories, rapid growth does not arise from equilibrium, but from disequilibrium. It relies on transferring 'dormant' assets (unexploited land) into the real economy, financed by debt. This mechanism rapidly enriches a country, but it has an absolute limit: when the stock of easily mobilisable land or infrastructure is exhausted, the machine stops.

**The Historical Burden — 2,000 Years of Fatal Cycles**

Applying this mechanism to Chinese history reveals a recurring tragedy. Over two millennia, the empire experienced seven major collapses (from the Han dynasty to the Republic). Each time, the scenario was the same: when the elites monopolised all the land, the peasants were suffocated until violent revolt broke out. The empire had no peaceful means of restarting its economic engine; only destruction could restart the economy.

**The Western Escape Route and Its Limits — Banking and Industrial Hijacking**

The West temporarily 'hijacked' this fatal cycle thanks to new resources to exploit (mineral deposits, colonies) and by using banking to create money. But having exhausted its physical resources, it has been stuck since the 1970s in the creation of fictitious wealth (financial bubbles).

**China's 2018 Deadlock — The Nodal Point Reached**

From 1978 to 2018, China replicated this Western model on a continental scale, using municipal land sales (Tudi Caizheng) to finance its frenetic development. In 2018, the available land supply ran out: the wall had been hit. Faced with this impasse, the hope that artificial intelligence, data or carbon can replace the property sector is an illusion: these sectors do not create the mass employment required for 1.4 billion Chinese people.

**The Digital Palliative — Buying Time**

To avert a social explosion in the face of this deadline, the Chinese state did not resort to Western-style fictitious finance. Instead, it deployed an algorithmic control system on an unprecedented scale (social credit, e-CNY, flow regulation). This is not an end in itself, but a palliative measure to 'freeze' the social situation whilst waiting to find a genuine, tangible solution.

**The Real Solution — The Territorial Regeneration Rent (TRR)**

This solution is already being implemented on the ground. Instead of continuing to pave over virgin land, the state is injecting massive amounts of credit to regenerate land degraded by forty years of hyper-industrialisation, as seen in the Lishui pilot zone with the Two Mountains Bank. By restoring forests, soils and waterways, China is creating an entirely new economic sector. Ecological restoration is becoming the new driving force behind local job creation.

**TRR as the Foundation of a New Diplomacy — The Diplomacy of Regeneration**

Beyond China, TRR is laying the foundations for a new form of diplomacy. By demonstrating that it is possible to move beyond the Nodal Point without massive destruction of wealth, China is offering the Global South a structural alternative to Western imperialism. Instead of exporting an extractive model (logistical colonisation, factories vulnerable to the 'sovereignty rent' of host states), China can export a model of topological regeneration: the Two Mountains Bank, GEP in KPIs, and Regenerative Usufruct Certificates. This 'Two Mountains diplomacy' positions the Celestial Economic Way (Tiandao Jingji, 天道经济) as a new standard of sustainable civilisation, an alternative to the exhausted Western model.

Formally, the projection of the operator $T_{\mathrm{mixed}}$ onto the spectral sheets of partner countries enables each state in the Global South, stuck in its own Nodal Point, to trigger its own cycle of ecological regeneration. This export of the model — via the Two Mountains Bank, the integration of the GEP into local KPIs, and Regenerative Usufruct Certificates — constitutes "Two Mountains diplomacy", an alternative to the logistical imperialism of the Hardened Enclaves.

**Strategic Conclusion — The Mathematical Formalisation of a Process Already Underway**

China has already identified Stasis and begun implementing territorial regeneration mechanisms — in Lishui, Sanming, Ganzhou and Hainan. This work proposes a mathematical formalisation of this diagnosis and this solution.

The TRR is an operational application of discrete topology to the regulation of an economic system in crisis, whilst adhering to the laws of the 64→20 invariant. It offers China a peaceful way out to avoid the tenth dynastic decline — the first time in two millennia that a Chinese state has had a tool to break the fatal cycle of dynastic collapses.

The collapse of the USSR sent shockwaves through the CCP: survive at all costs; do not suffer the same fate. This obsession, combined with a millennia-old historical experience, led the CCP-State to diagnose Stasis and to implement the TRR. It thus acts in harmony with the deep laws that govern complex systems.

# FOREWORD — The Two Audiences

This document is aimed at two different audiences.

**Level 1 — The Diagnosis and the Solution (Parts I and III)**

Intended for decision-makers, strategists, and political and economic leaders.

These parts present:

- **Part I**: The analysis of China's structural crisis — reaching the Nodal Point, Stasis, the impasse of the immaterial. No equations. No formalism. An economic, historical and strategic narrative.
- **Part III**: Territorial Regeneration Rent (TRR) as an operational solution — the mechanisms already at work in Lishui, Sanming, Ganzhou and Hainan. An analysis in terms of endogenous regulation, ecological restoration and territorial reindustrialisation.

Readers without a background in mathematics can read these sections without missing any of the essential points.

**Level 2 — The Topological Model and Its Implications (Parts II and IV)**

Intended for theorists, mathematicians and epistemologists.

These sections present:

- **Part II**: The mathematical formalisation of the model — Clifford algebra Cl(6,6), the 144 economic pentads, the 7 spectral thresholds, the transition operator T, and spectral observables. Here, the reader will find the rigorous foundations of the diagnosis set out in Part I.
- **Part IV**: The philosophical and systemic implications — the economy as an exosomatisation of topology, Chinese correlative thinking as a proto-model, divination as a pre-modern regulatory system.

Readers without a background in mathematics may skip these sections without losing the thread of the practical demonstration.

**A Mirrored Architecture**

| **Level** | **Part** | **Content** |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Diagnosis | **Part I** | The Chinese Stasis — historical and strategic narrative |
| Model | **Part II** | The Cl(6,6) topology — mathematical formalism |
| Solution | **Part III** | The TRR — operational application |
| Unification | **Part IV** | Philosophical and cultural implications |

Parts I and III should be read consecutively. Parts II and IV provide further insight for readers wishing to understand the theoretical foundations and epistemological implications.

# PART I — DIAGNOSIS: CHINESE STASIS

## Introduction

### 1. The observation: continuous models fail to predict crises

Since the publication of Keynes's *General Theory* (1936), macroeconomics has developed increasingly sophisticated mathematical tools. DSGE (Dynamic Stochastic General Equilibrium) models, rational expectations models and Monte Carlo simulations have become the standard tools of central banks and finance ministries. Yet these models have failed to predict the major crises of the 20th and 21st centuries: the Great Depression (1929), the oil shock (1973), the subprime crisis (2008), and, more recently, post-COVID inflation (2022) [@keynes1936; @fourastie1979].

This failure is not accidental. It is structural. Conventional models treat the economy as a continuous space, where variables (prices, wages, output, interest rates) adjust through convergence mechanisms towards a supposed equilibrium. However, the **Theory of Rent Extraction (ToRE)**, developed by Thierry Rebour (2000, 2011), has shown that economic growth is fundamentally a phenomenon of structural disequilibrium, governed by conservation laws and discrete breaking points: the **Nodal Points** [@rebour2000; @rebour2011].

### 2. The hypothesis: the economy is a discrete information system

To bridge this gap, we propose the following hypothesis:

> The economy is a discrete information system, whose configuration space is isomorphic to the Clifford algebra $\mathrm{Cl}(6,0)$, and whose dynamics are governed by a universal invariant: the $64 \rightarrow 20$ [@Rowlands2007; @DeDominicis_2026_Tian_Dao_AI].

This hypothesis rests on three pillars:

- **The formalism of Clifford algebras and nilpotents**, developed by Peter Rowlands (2007, 2014). Rowlands demonstrated that the Clifford algebra $\mathrm{Cl}(6,0)$ decomposes into 12 pentads (sets of 5 elements), and that this pentad structure is fundamental to both physics (the Dirac equation) and biology [@Rowlands2007; @Rowlands2014]. Our model transposes this architecture to economics: six binary dimensions (inflation, purchasing power, profits, buy-back, dispersion, land tenure) generate $2^6 = 64$ possible states, exactly like the spin space in quantum physics, the codon space in biology, or the hexagram space of the *Yi Jing*.

- **The substrate-independent generalisation**, due to De Dominicis (2026). De Dominicis extended Rowlands' model by showing that the structure $\mathrm{Cl}(6,0)$ and the invariant $64 \rightarrow 20$ are not specific to physics or biology, but constitute a universal property of any complex system, regardless of its substrate [@DeDominicis_2026_Tian_Dao_AI; @DeDominicis_2026_WuXing]. It is this generalisation that allows the concept to be applied to economics.

- **The exceptional lattice $\Lambda_{72}$**, constructed by Gabriele Nebe (2010) [@Nebe2010; @NebeSloane]. Nebe discovered a pair of unimodular lattices of dimension 72, whose 48 non-degenerate roots provide the fundamental resonance frequencies of complex systems. De Dominicis has shown that these 48 roots project onto a basis of 15 universal constants ($\beta_k$), which are the same for nuclear physics, chemistry, biology and social systems [@DeDominicis_2026_Arithmetic].

### 3. The $64 \rightarrow 20$ invariant: a universal law

The idea of a $64 \rightarrow 20$ invariant is not new. It appears in several fields, as so many manifestations of the same topological law [@Rowlands2007; @Hill_Rowlands_2007; @DeDominicis_2026_Tian_Dao_AI]:

## Chapter 1 – The Foundations of the Theory of Rent Extraction (ToRE)

Developed by Thierry Rebour [@rebour2000; @rebour2011], the ToRE models the sequence by which societies transform latent wealth into monetary wealth. It demonstrates that the power of growth rests on an initial structural asymmetry between two distinct conceptual spaces [@guieysse2016].

### 1.1. Ontology of Value and Wealth

The ToRE divides the economy into two spheres of radically different nature [@rebour2000; @rebour2011]:

- **The Space of Value** does not refer simply to unvalued assets, but to assets kept out of the market by symbolic foundations and for reasons that are more anthropological than economic. This wealth is 'frozen' by sacralisation, collective beliefs or foundational taboos that confer ontological depth upon these spaces [@desmarais2000; @ritchotmercier1992].

- **The Space of Wealth** refers to the monetised economic circuit, where these same assets, stripped of their symbolic veil, become factors of production that generate prices, wages and profits [@rebour2000].

**Interpretation for China:** Within the Chinese imperial matrix, land was the absolute Space of Value [@zhao2006tudi; @schmitt2017sol]. During the Maoist era, collectivised land constituted a latent Space of Value of colossal proportions. In the *Tudi Caizheng* model (1978–2018), rural land, frozen by the *Hukou* system, constituted a Space of Value which the state gradually transferred to the Space of Wealth [@zhou2023liweilii; @chan2009hukou].

### 1.2. The mechanism of Rent Extraction and money creation

Rent Extraction is the act of transferring an asset from the Space of Value to the Space of Wealth. This transfer requires a political or social decision that lifts the original prohibition [@rebour2000].

However, as the asset is not yet productive at the time of transfer, the purchaser does not have the necessary income to pay for it. Financing can therefore only take place through monetary anticipation: Rent Extraction is intrinsically linked to the creation of money through credit. As the theory stipulates, material Rent Extraction is always 'made solvent by an act of money creation, in other words, a loan' [@rebour2011].

**Interpretation for China:** LGFVs (Local Government Financing Vehicles) served as the instrument for this credit based on monetary anticipation. By borrowing heavily from state-owned banks, local authorities created the money required for land buy-backs [@zhou2023liweilii; @chen2024; @mof2023lgfv].

### 1.3. Exogenous demand in advance

The injection of credit intended for land acquisition does not flow into the real economy at the same time as the new assets come into production: it precedes it. This **exogenous demand in advance** results in new purchasing power (compensation, construction wages, land profits) being injected before the new means of production are operational [@rebour2000].

This structural lag generates price inflation (particularly in land prices) which rises faster than labour incomes. Growth is thus fundamentally out of balance or polarised: it structurally favours rent seekers at the expense of wage earners [@rebour2011; @rebour2011a].

### 1.4. Increasing returns and territorial multipliers

Although it arises from an imbalance, this sequence generates real prosperity. Exogenous demand concentrates capital and economic activity in a single location. This spatial concentration generates positive externalities (networks, know-how, infrastructure) that increase overall productivity. The profits generated are reinvested, creating multiplier effects [@rebour2000; @desmarais2000].

### 1.5. The substitution of externalities

This process of concentration is not without spatial costs; it is governed by a strict **law of externality substitution** [@rebour2000; @desmarais2000; @ritchotmercier1992]. To understand how this works, we must go back to its historical roots. This phenomenon is not simply a matter of a monetary transfer, but stems from a productivity shock — initially in agriculture — which made it viable to relocate productive tasks to the countryside. This is the very principle of the *Verlagsystem* (contract manufacturing system): production is outsourced to rural areas, whilst the city retains strategic control (design, financing, marketing) and captures the bulk of the surplus value generated. Consequently, metropolitan prosperity is by no means a form of endogenous growth: it is the accounting reflection of the impoverishment of the peripheries [@rebour2011a].

### 1.6. The Nodal Point

As the ToRE is governed by laws of conservation (Value is not created *ex nihilo*; it merely shifts), the cycle of unbalanced growth is limited by the **Nodal Point** [@rebour2011]. This systemic threshold is reached when the stock of readily mobilisable Value runs out. The flow of credit intended for Rent Extraction dries up, depriving the economy of its 'advance exogenous demand'. Inflation slows, asset prices stagnate, and increasing returns give way to decreasing returns [@rebour2000; @rebour2011].

### 1.7. The Law of Conservation and Centrifugal Dynamics

At the Nodal Point, the crisis is not an anomaly, but the system's mandatory readjustment mechanism: to recreate Value, Wealth must be destroyed (bankruptcies, deindustrialisation, price collapses) [@rebour2011]. The ToRE emphasises an often-overlooked morphological corollary: reaching the Nodal Point reverses spatial dynamics [@rebour2011a; @desmarais2000]. The period of growth polarised space towards the centre (centripetal dynamics). At the Nodal Point, these dynamics become centrifugal. We observe diffuse peri-urbanisation, the de-urbanisation of centres, and a reversal of urban hierarchies. The crisis is therefore as much financial as it is morphological [@ritchotmercier1992].

## Chapter 2 — The Chinese imperial matrix: a single, centuries-long trend, from antiquity to the terminal Nodal Point

### Introduction — A single trend, two modes of Value

The history of imperial China is traditionally interpreted through the prism of Confucian morality (the virtue of the sovereign), palace intrigues or the analysis of social classes. However, if we set aside the chronological narrative to apply the framework of the Theory of Rent Extraction (ToRE), the two millennia of dynastic cycles appear as the repetitive unfolding of an inexorable economic cycle [@elvin1973pattern; @zhao2006tudi; @perkins1969agricultural]. The succession of Chinese dynasties does not correspond to a succession of independent economic trends. From the emergence of agrarian civilisation to contemporary China, this is a single, multi-century trend, of which the various dynasties constitute successive historical configurations.

The fundamental mechanism of this trend is based on the law of growth in disequilibrium [@rebour2000]. The process of buy-back transfers land (or labour) from the realm of Value to the realm of Wealth, in exchange for a monetary payment (loan) that draws on future production. This influx of 'exogenous' demand, which runs ahead of the productive cycle, drives prices up faster than incomes, generating an inflationary growth trend. In this polarised regime, profits and rent reinforce one another, underpinned by increasing rates of return, whilst the purchasing power of wages tends to be eroded.

The functioning of the system then depends on the relative scarcity of two fundamental modes of value: land value and the value of servile labour. When labour is scarcer than land, the system finds itself in a pioneering phase. When land becomes scarcer, the system shifts towards land rent. Chinese history can thus be understood as a succession of shifts between these two modes, accompanied by avoidance mechanisms designed to postpone the arrival of the Nodal Point.

### 2.1. The two fundamental modes of value

#### 2.1.1. The land-based mode and agrarian ontology

To understand the application of the ToRE to China, one must grasp its fundamental 'agrarian ontology': unlike the West, which has historically sanctified trade and then industry, Chinese civilisation has sanctified the land. True wealth does not arise from commercial exchange, but from the cultivation of the soil [@granet1922religion; @schmitt2017sol; @zhao2006tudi].

Within this paradigm, the land is not merely a factor of production; it is the absolute Space of Value. But for a rent to be extracted from it, this land must be kept outside the free market and subject to a political prohibition. It is here that China innovated well before the industrial era: through draconian control of peasant mobility (the conceptual precursors of the *Hukou*). By tying peasants to the land, the imperial state secured a captive labour force and a perfectly stable stock of Value [@fei2013xiangtu; @vandermeersch1980wangdao].

In a sedentary society, access to land becomes the main factor of economic differentiation, enabling the extraction of a rent. This phenomenon has a key consequence: the wealth produced by labour does not disappear, but an increasing share of this wealth is transferred to the holder of land rights. The system thus tends towards concentration.

#### 2.1.2. The servile mode

The situation is radically different when land is relatively abundant but labour is scarce. The capture of an external population drastically alters this situation. The slave constitutes a particular form of Value: he represents a labour force whose productive capacity is directly owned by the holder. Conquest makes it possible to supply the system with a servile labour force. The frontier appears as a configuration in which territorial expansion and the expansion of the labour force stock operate in tandem.

### 2.2. The two crisis-avoidance mechanisms and the Nodal Point

The dynamics of rent extraction generate growth that inevitably comes up against the limits of solvent demand, threatening to trigger crises of overproduction. To prevent a collapse in prices, the system has two avoidance mechanisms:

- **The first, spatial avoidance**, involves pushing back the system's boundaries through geographical expansion or export. Chinese history offers a paradigmatic illustration of these avoidance loops: one province exports to another to avoid local overproduction. But when the loop closes, with goods having circulated throughout the provinces, there is nowhere left to export to. This marks the onset of the global crisis.

- **The second, temporal avoidance**, postpones the constraint into the future through the use of money, credit and debt. However, this stratagem does not create any new real resources. The gap widens between monetary value and the productive base, paving the way for financial crises.

These mechanisms make it possible to postpone the Nodal Point. In theory, this occurs when all land is on the market, but in the reality of unbalanced economies, it occurs as soon as more than half of the stock of Value has been bought up and Wealth (production) exceeds Value. As prices are no longer driven by the influx of value, they stagnate. To restore rising prices, a Destruction of Wealth and a corresponding recreation of Value are essential. Returns then begin to decline: the economy becomes a zero-sum game, marking the onset of a prolonged 'crisis' [@rebour2011; @elvin1973pattern].

### 2.3. The spatial laws of Rent Extraction: urban form and the substitution of externalities

The relationship between modes of value and the economic climate determines spatial organisation. An inflationary growth trend generates centripetal dynamics and increasing returns that polarise space.

In a frontier situation, cities adopt an orthogonal geometry (rectangular grid), as seen in ancient China, in Rome, or under the Tang and Yuan dynasties. According to the Naba-Chen theory, these grid plans introduced by nomadic peoples reflect the influence of a servile mode reintroduced through conquest [@shatzman-steinhardt1988].

When the frontier closes and land becomes scarce, the logic changes. To maintain profits in the face of insufficient domestic purchasing power, developed cities must maximise their exports. Spatial attractors then become axial: towns expand in 'arms' along transport routes. This is the law of externalities substitution: value is transferred from the countryside to the towns. Land grabbing did not result from brute force, but from the structural asymmetry of agricultural markets: the forced indebtedness of peasants in the face of a rigid tax system, coupled with the 'price scissors' imposed by the elites monopolising grain storage, systematically transferred wealth from the countryside to the centres [@rebour2011a; @huang1974taxation].

### 2.4. Ancient China: from available land to rent

China's growth truly began with the Shang dynasty (around 2500 BC). In the early stages, the available land allowed for the maintenance of a frontier. However, population growth and the integration of territories reduced this margin. China then entered a system of land rent. This transformation marked the point at which the trend became entrenched. Political crises were no longer the end of a system, but manifestations of the same problem: how to continue land acquisition when land resources became insufficient?

### 2.5. Qin and Han: centralisation, land registration and the locking in of the trend

The Han dynasty (c. 300 BC to AD 300) marked a structural turning point. Land buy-ups accelerated. The Han dynasty abolished slavery, introduced a rigorous land survey system and closed the frontier. Value shifted structurally towards land. This transition is evident in the physical landscape: towns ceased to be laid out in a grid pattern, a sign of the abandonment of the geometry of the frontier in favour of a rent-seeking polarisation. Land concentration gave rise to the tensions that would lead to the crisis at the end of the Han dynasty (the Yellow Turban Rebellion in 184), the first major manifestation of the exhaustion of the land tenure system [@elvin1973pattern; @zhao2006tudi].

### 2.6. The Sixteen Kingdoms (4th–5th centuries): destruction of wealth and spatial division

The fall of the Han dynasty and the Three Kingdoms period marked the emergence of the Nodal Point: extreme land concentration, a collapse in demand and a crisis of overproduction. To emerge from this crisis, the system resorted to massive Destruction of Wealth through civil wars. However, it was the incursion of nomadic peoples, known as the 'Five Barbarians' (*Wu Hu*), in the 4th century, that enabled the trend to be reignited.

The conquest of northern China by these non-Chinese peoples brought about a violent redistribution of the Space of Value. The sedentary landowning elites were decimated or fled southwards, leading to a split in the empire. In the north, the new nomadic rulers founded the Sixteen Kingdoms and subsequently the Northern Dynasties. They reintroduced the servile mode on a massive scale: the Chinese peasant populations were often enslaved or bound to the land in a semi-servile status to cultivate the estates of the nomadic military aristocracies. The frontier and Value through labour were reactivated.

In the South, the Southern Dynasties (Eastern Jin, Liu Song, etc.) exploited a spatial avoidance strategy by pushing the agricultural frontier towards the Yangtze regions, bringing new lands into cultivation. This geographical division from the 4th to the 5th century illustrates a solution to the crisis: the North slipped into the servile mode through conquest, whilst the South sought a new frontier for land expansion.

### 2.7. Nomadic invasions and the reactivation of the frontier

When the land tenure system reaches a high level of concentration, it always eventually implodes under the weight of overproduction and falling prices. The Chinese state attempts to control prices, which prevents wages from rising quickly enough. Driven by greed, the state and the aristocracy raise taxes. Excessive corruption prevents the economy from recovering, and inequalities lead to looting and civil wars.

It is here that the dynamics of nomadic invasions play a role in macro-historical regulation. Every new dynasty is born from the ashes of a Destruction of Wealth. The new regime carries out a vast redistribution of land confiscated from the former elites. The Space of Value is suddenly redistributed [@huang1974taxation; @yang2015tangdai]. The nomads invade China, seize power, redistribute the land and reignite a trend of growth. Their arrival brings about a Destruction of Wealth (which recreates Value) and reintroduces a servile labour force. The pioneer front reappears temporarily. With the Sui and Tang dynasties, the cycle begins anew. The nomads become Sinicised, adopt agrarian institutions and turn into exploiters: land concentration begins once more.

### 2.8. Tang and Song: expansion, grid-like organisation and saturation of space

The Tang re-established a powerful empire. The Silk Road constituted a vast episode of spatial avoidance. The urban grid re-emerged in Xi'an/Chang'an and Luoyang (7th century). But expansion reached its limits: land constraints resurfaced with the An Lushan rebellion (755) [@twitchett1970financial; @yang2015tangdai].

Under the Song dynasty, land was structurally scarce and spatial avoidance proved insufficient. The system shifted massively towards temporal avoidance. Fiat currency (*jiaozi*) and maritime trade mobilised future wealth. But currency does not create land. The gap between monetary value and the real economic base erupted into financial crises.

### 2.9. The Jurchen (Jin Dynasty, 1120–1250): the split of the empire and the duality of modes of value

Prior to the Mongol unification, China experienced a new split that illustrates the duality of mechanisms for avoiding the ToRE. In the 12th century, the Jurchen (Jin Dynasty, 1115–1234, ruling from 1120 to 1250) seized northern China, bringing an end to the Northern Song in 1127.

This invasion once again led to a Destruction of Wealth in the North. The Jurchen, a nomadic people, imposed a mixed system: they confiscated the lands of the Chinese elites to allocate them to their military aristocracy and reduced a section of the peasantry to a semi-servile status (the *meng'an* and *mouke* systems). The pioneer front and the servile mode were thus reactivated in the northern half of the country.

The South (the Southern Song, 1127–1279), deprived of territorial expansion, entered a phase of hyper-specialisation in temporal avoidance. The regime's survival depended on an explosion in fiat currency, credit and massive maritime exports (porcelain, silk) to compensate for the loss of the 'Space of Value'. China found itself divided between a North (Jin) based on forced labour and a South (Song) suffocating under debt and monetary inflation. It was upon this exhausted duality that the Mongols (Yuan) would impose their unification through widespread destruction of wealth.

### 2.10. Yuan and Ming: from the return of the frontier to institutional stagnation

The Mongol (Yuan) conquest reintegrated China into a vast Eurasian empire, reviving the frontier (the grid system of Beijing in the 13th century). But the conquerors settled down, became Sinicised, and land rent regained the upper hand [@elvin1973pattern].

The Ming inherited an overburdened system. Temporal avoidance gained prominence with the influx of foreign silver, but did not alter the amount of land available. Above all, the Ming introduced a fatal rigidity: they refused to sell land as freehold to the peasants, which would have enabled the revival of land repurchase. This rigidity led to institutional stagnation. Peasant indebtedness and land concentration reached their peak with Li Zicheng's rebellion and the Manchu invasion [@huang1974taxation].

### 2.11. The Qing, the Republic and the Mao era: from the final spatial frontier to the stagnation of the trend

The Qing Dynasty carried out the last major territorial expansion (Xinjiang, Tibet, Taiwan), the final major episode of spatial avoidance. But in the 19th century, external space became insufficient. Land constraints once again became overwhelming (the Taiping Rebellion) and Western pressures imposed structural debt [@perkins1969agricultural]. The Nodal Point of the imperial matrix was reached.

The collapse of 1912 and the establishment of the Republic did not create a new trend: fragmentation, hyperinflation and the absence of a rent extraction mechanism characterised the terminal crisis [@elvin1973pattern].

The Mao era constituted a political break, but ushered in an **artificial stasis** in the sense of the Theory of Rent Extraction. Collectivisation removed land from the market and the *Hukou* system restricted mobility. The rent extraction system was frozen. Land and labour became stocks of latent value, the mobilisation of which was institutionally prevented.

### 2.12. Deng Xiaoping: the acceleration of the trend and the mobilisation of latent stock

The 1978 reforms represent the reactivation of the old trend. The difference lies in the speed at which the latent value accumulated over centuries is mobilised.

Urbanisation is transforming vast swathes of rural land into urban property. The retention of the *Hukou* system makes it possible to control the mobility of a cheap labour force, thereby sustaining rent and profits. Land becomes the vehicle for a colossal conversion of value, financed by massive temporal deferral (credit, LGFV). China is abruptly accelerating the old trend to its very limit.

### 2.13. Summary of the nine declines: the standardised dynastic matrix

Analysis of the nine major periods of decline confirms the validity of this model whilst revealing two adjustment variables: labour scarcity and exogenous shocks [@elvin1973pattern; @perkins1969agricultural; @twitchett1970financial; @huang1974taxation]. The following table summarises the application of the standardised dynastic sequence (sovereign rent extraction → substitution of externalities → Nodal Point) to Chinese imperial history:
