### 2.14. The Terminal Nodal Point and the Trimorphism of Chinese Space

The contemporary period is characterised by the simultaneous saturation of both possibilities for avoidance. Spatial avoidance no longer has a frontier; temporal avoidance (debt) can no longer replace a real basis of Value. China is reaching its terminal Nodal Point.

Chinese geographical space today reflects this situation of diminishing returns through the **trimorphism of space** [@rebour2011]:

- **Metropolitan areas** (hypertrophied): the intensity of capital still manages to overcome the constraint of rent (vertical expansion), polarising selective global demand.
- **Urban areas**: where ground rent hinders the growth of cities in proportion to their size.
- **Rural areas**: impoverished by the substitution of externalities for the benefit of metropolitan areas, victims of the atrophy of their capital.

In previous crises, the destruction of wealth (civil war, invasion) made it possible to alter the ratio of scarcity. Today, this possibility is structurally limited. The repetition of the old mechanism would lead to stasis, in a zero-sum game.

### 2.15. Territorial Regeneration Rent: breaking the trend

It is in this context that the **Territorial Regeneration Rent (TRR)** takes on its meaning.

In the old system, Value is obtained by mobilising a resource that is already available. At the Nodal Point, the only way out for capital is the Destruction of Wealth in order to recreate Value. The TRR offers an alternative: the regeneration of a degraded space itself becomes a generator of Value.

Space becomes an object capable of qualitative transformation. The TRR would therefore not be yet another instalment in the spiral of avoidance, but the first attempt to break free from the historical trend based on scarcity and the appropriation of existing resources, replacing destruction with an endogenous creation of value.

### Conclusion — The Nodal Point as a historical rupture

Chinese history appears as a unique trajectory of oscillation between land-based value and servile value, punctuated by the substitution of externalities and mechanisms of avoidance. For centuries, dynastic crises and nomadic invasions made it possible to destroy wealth in order to recreate value and postpone the Nodal Point.

Mao suspended the mechanism through institutional stagnation. Deng reactivated it at an unprecedented pace, consuming the last reserves of latent value. The contemporary period corresponds to the simultaneous exhaustion of both spatial and temporal avoidance.

The historical question is no longer how to restart the next cycle through destruction, but whether it is possible to break out of the trend itself. This is precisely the theoretical function that the Territorial Regeneration Rent could fulfil. Contemporary China therefore faces a historic choice: to continue the spiral of avoidance until Stasis, or to transform the very principle of rent extraction by generating Qualitative Value.

---

## Chapter 3 – The Western Interruption: the banking and industrial loophole

Whilst imperial China spent two millennia repeating a closed land cycle, the West found, for a time, a way to circumvent the laws of the Theory of Rent Extraction (ToRE). By observing the mechanism of the Nodal Point — the depletion of the stock of Value leading to the Destruction of Wealth — Europe and then America developed a strategy of systematic avoidance. They succeeded in postponing the fatal deadline by artificially broadening the very definition of what could be 'redeemed', and by inventing the financial machinery capable of making this broadening financially viable [@rebour2000; @elvin1973pattern; @fourastie1979].

### 3.1. Industrial Rent Extraction: the sudden expansion of the stock of Value

In the Chinese imperial matrix, the Space of Value was finite and bounded: arable land. Once all the land had been redistributed and then monopolised, the Nodal Point was inevitable [@elvin1973pattern; @rebour2000].

The Western innovation of the Industrial Revolution was not so much technological as economic: it consisted of breaking down the barriers to new Spaces of Value. The subsoil (coal, then oil and minerals) and the machine itself were 'frozen' assets, either due to the technical impossibility of exploiting them or the absence of clearly defined property rights [@rebour2000; @fourastie1979].

Industrial capitalisation was the political and economic act of transferring assets from the 'Space of Value' to the 'Space of Wealth'. Unlike Chinese land, which was static, this new Value was dynamic: technological innovation was constantly creating new underground frontiers or new machines to be acquired. This shift gave the West the illusion of having overcome diminishing returns [@rebour2011; @fourastie1979].

### 3.2. International banking as a means of creating solvency

However, the ToRE lays down an inviolable rule: the transfer of an asset to the Wealth Space requires 'exogenous demand in advance'. Yet, at the time of building a factory or opening a coal mine, the asset is not yet productive. Who pays? [@rebour2000]

This is where the financial sector comes into play. The West pioneered the institutionalisation of money creation through private credit (and subsequently international banking). The major banking houses in London, Amsterdam and later New York provided the monetary advance necessary for industrial capital accumulation. The money lent to build infrastructure or factories was spent immediately (on wages and profits), creating the structural inflation characteristic of the polarised ToRE system [@rebour2011; @griffin2010creature].

But to prevent this system from collapsing under the weight of its own inflation, it constantly required new markets to absorb production and balance out imbalances. This mechanism for externalising costs, initially confined to the European countryside, was removed from its original geographical context by international finance. In the 19th century, international finance funded the transfer of production and the extraction of raw materials to the 'global countryside' constituted by the colonies [@rebour2011a; @griffin2010creature].

### 3.3. The first global Nodal Point (1914–1945)

By virtue of the law of conservation, the expansion of the Space of Value by industry and finance could only be a temporary phenomenon. By the end of the 19th century, the entire Earth had been surveyed, mapped and colonised. There were no longer any 'unclaimed lands' or unclaimed subsoils to fuel the machine of Rent Extraction. The global stock of Value suddenly reached saturation point, signifying the attainment of a Nodal Point on a civilisational scale [@rebour2011; @fourastie1979].

The ToRE thus explains what traditional historiography strives to treat as mere diplomatic incidents: the First World War, the Great Depression and the Second World War were, in reality, the inevitable and violent adjustments of the global financial and industrial system at this critical threshold. The Law of Rent Extraction demanded a massive destruction of wealth in order to recreate value [@rebour2011; @lewin1996report].

The West had temporarily diverted the cycle by expanding it to a global scale. But by closing it off again (once colonisation was complete), it bore the full brunt of the law of conservation which, for centuries, had reduced Chinese dynasties to ashes. The only difference was that this time the adjustment took place on the scale of the entire industrial civilisation [@rebour2011; @fourastie1979].

---

## Chapter 4 – China 1978–2018: The Golden Age of Material Rent Extraction

China's take-off from the 1990s onwards illustrates the ToRE sequence: a vast stock of off-market Land Value, the lifting of the ban and the triggering of Rent Extraction through advance credit, and the generation of increasing returns.

### 4.1. The 1978 reform and the lifting of the Maoist ban

To launch a cycle of Rent Extraction, a vast stock of frozen value is required. Under the Maoist era (the period prior to the reforms), land and the means of production were collectivised. They were not exploited according to market laws, nor were they sanctified by an ancestral anthropological taboo: they were simply 'frozen' by inefficient planning, constituting a Space of Latent Value of colossal proportions [@chan2009hukou; @zhao2006tudi].

Deng Xiaoping did not privatise the land (which would have undermined political control); instead, he lifted the economic taboo whilst maintaining the political one. The *Hukou* (household registration system) played here the role that peasant status played under the Tang or Qing dynasties: it artificially kept the rural labour force and the land within the Value Space, preventing their free trading on the market [@chan2009hukou; @zhang2012model].

The reform consisted of authorising the transfer of usage rights (not ownership) of collectively owned land to farmers or local authorities. This is an unprecedented form of Rent Extraction: China has mobilised the land without relinquishing full ownership, creating the conditions for a captive land rent for the state [@zhao2006tudi; @long2018chuantong].

### 4.2. The Hukou as a political instrument of the Buy-back

The mechanism of the buy-back requires a stock of value that is politically frozen. The fundamental specificity of the Chinese model lies in the architecture of its property regime, a duality often misunderstood by Western analytical frameworks. As the political theorist Zhang Weiwei points out, the Chinese model is based on the fact that 'the state owns the bulk of resources, from minerals to land, but the right to use this land is flexible and shaped by market forces' [@chan2009hukou].

However, for the 'flexibility' of the right of use to generate maximum rent at the time of rent extraction, the state's ownership of the original stock had to be strictly locked in. It was precisely at this microscopic level that the *Hukou* came into play [@chan2009hukou]. By linking social rights (health, education, housing) to one's rural place of origin and prohibiting permanent migration to urban areas, the system artificially kept rural land and labour within the Value Space.

When the local authorities subsequently decided to initiate the Buy-Back, the purchase price of this rural land — deprived of any legal liquidity by the *Hukou* — was kept at a level close to zero. This artificial disparity made it possible to maximise the rent captured by the state upon the resale of usage rights to property developers, thereby initiating the cycle of exogenous demand.

This capture of land rent was accompanied by an equally decisive demographic externalisation. Through the *Hukou* lockdown, the cost of the social reproduction of the labour force was shifted to the countryside. Migrant workers, deprived of access to urban public services, relied structurally on families who had remained in the village to care for children, the sick and the elderly. The village therefore not only provided a supply of rural land to be expropriated at zero marginal cost; it also subsidised the cost of reproducing the urban workforce free of charge. This dual levy – on land and on social services – made it possible to keep construction wages at an artificially low level, thereby maximising the rent captured by the state via the LGFVs and accelerating the cycle of exogenous demand.

### 4.3. The CCP State as the central bank of the Rent Extraction: the LGFVs

In the Western model (Part I, Chapter 3), private banks created money to finance the industrial Rent Extraction, capturing a share of the rent in the process. China has bypassed this process. The CCP State has become its own banker [@rebour2000].

Unable to borrow directly from the markets (or only very little at first), local authorities created ad hoc financial vehicles: the LGFVs (Local Government Financing Vehicles) 城投债 (*Chengtouzhai*). These entities, implicitly guaranteed by the state, borrowed heavily from state-owned banks [@mof2023lgfv; @imf2023china; @chen2024].

#### 4.3.1. Physical rent extraction and the collectivisation of rent

This money was used to expropriate peasants (at a price close to zero, thanks to the *Hukou* freeze) and to finance infrastructure. The sums paid to peasants and construction workers immediately fuelled the real economy: this is the pure realisation of the "advance exogenous demand" theorised by the ToRE. The resale of usage rights by the CCP-led state generated colossal profits, which were used to repay the LGFVs and to finance new projects. The cycle of unbalanced growth (where land price inflation outstripped wage growth) was set in motion.

It is, however, essential to distinguish this recycling of rent from imperial predation or Western fictitious finance. Unlike the dynastic elites who hoarded agrarian rent, or the financial markets which channel it into speculative assets, the CCP State collectivised this rent to convert it into territorial capital. Mega-infrastructure projects (high-speed rail, dams, energy transmission networks) are not a by-product of growth: they are its physical manifestation, realising the increasing returns and territorial multipliers of the Theory of Rent. This tangible translation of land rent into public goods explains the model's enduring legitimacy.

Nevertheless, the law of conservation implies that, in doing so, the State was quietly laying the groundwork for its own Nodal Point. By building this infrastructure ahead of actual needs in order to support exogenous demand, it accumulated a colossal stock of assets which, in the long run, risked turning into financial liabilities if they failed to generate the future revenue necessary to repay the LGFV debt.

#### 4.3.2. The structural impasse: the LGFV crisis

LGFVs are the central instrument of this Rent Extraction, but also its weakness. Created by local governments to circumvent the ban on direct borrowing, they have backed their debt against the future value of land. This mechanism rests on a fundamental contradiction: local authorities are both the masters and the prisoners of their LGFVs. They control them (through shareholding, the appointment of directors, and access to land collateral), but they are bound by the implicit guarantee that obliges them to support them come what may — all the more so as the fall in land revenue has dried up their main source of repayment.

To conceal the fact that the Nodal Point had been breached, the system generated a dual debt structure: official debt, recorded on balance sheets and subject to Beijing's quotas, and hidden debt, incurred off-balance-sheet. This hidden debt made it possible to artificially sustain credit flows for years, until the collapse of the property market brought it to light.

Faced with systemic risk, Beijing has launched a wide-ranging drive to regain control: strengthening supervision, banning implicit guarantees, and forcibly transforming the LGFVs into commercial entities. But this process comes up against an inescapable reality: the LGFVs are too heavily indebted to be abandoned (without triggering an immediate destruction of wealth), and too fragile to be rescued without creating massive moral hazard. It is precisely this impasse — the impossibility of allowing the system to collapse and the impossibility of maintaining it as it stands without sinking into fictitious wealth — that constitutes the concrete expression of the Chinese Nodal Point. The 'destruction of wealth' theorised by the ToRE takes the anticipated form here of a forced debt restructuring [@imf2023china; @mof2023lgfv].

Theoretically, Beijing has three conventional options for resolving the crisis and absorbing this excess wealth, but each proves structurally incapable of restarting the cycle:

- **Debt swaps (controlled evaporation):** This involves Beijing requiring state-owned banks to exchange their short-term receivables for very long-term, low-yield sovereign bonds. In ToRE terms, this constitutes a destruction of future income without the destruction of the physical asset. Whilst this solution preserves apparent capital, it freezes the system in a state of financial stasis: the banks, deprived of returns, lose their ability to finance new cycles, dragging the economy into a Japanese-style 'zombification' that does not resolve the Nodal Point but freezes it indefinitely.

- **Selective default (surgical destruction):** This approach would involve imposing a haircut on the creditors of certain LGFVs, or even allowing the most insolvent entities to fail. It is the purest form of Wealth Destruction — the modern, targeted equivalent of the peasant revolts of yesteryear. However, the risk of systemic contagion is significant: a partial default could trigger a widespread liquidity crisis, as investors withdraw their funds from the entire local financial system as a precaution.

- **Monetary inflation (dilutive erosion):** The People's Bank of China could create money on a massive scale to buy back LGFV debt, thereby eroding the value of wealth through dilution. But fleeing into inflation is the quintessential Western approach. In China, rises in retail or property prices have historically been the trigger for the collapse of dynasties. This is the ultimate political risk for the CCP state, capable of destroying its own legitimacy even before the Nodal Point is addressed.

Whilst the swap leads to Stasis, default to contagion, and inflation to social risk, these three paths share a fatal commonality: they are fruitless. They manage the evaporation of Wealth, but none of them recreates the stock of Value necessary to initiate a new cycle.

It is precisely this structural impasse that makes the activation of the **Territorial Regeneration Rent (TRR)** inevitable. To avoid the unproductive destruction of wealth, the State must force the emergence of a new cycle of Rent Extraction by backing existing debt with an underlying asset that generates real rent.

### 4.4. The Rent Extraction of Geography: mega-infrastructure and the substitution of externalities

China's genius during this period lay in understanding that the 'Space of Value' was not limited to agricultural land on the outskirts of cities (which eventually runs out). It applied the concept of Rent Extraction to physical geography itself [@zhang2018bilu; @rebour2000].

Western history had harnessed the subsoil and the colonies. China, unable to colonise, colonised its own landlocked interior. The mega-infrastructure projects of the 1990s and 2000s were the instruments of this geographical Rent Extraction:

#### 4.4.1. The Three Gorges Dam

The Yangtze River was not merely a transport route; it was a wild 'Space of Value', whose devastating floods represented a periodic destruction of Wealth, but whose energy and hydraulic potential remained entirely inaccessible due to the technical and financial impossibility of harnessing it.

The decision to build the dam was part of the pure mechanism of Rent Extraction. The State overrode the natural constraint through a massive political act, mobilising a colossal loan (over 200 billion yuan at the time) to 'buy' the riverbed. This injection of capital generated phenomenal "exogenous demand in advance" for nearly twenty years, supporting the national heavy industry (cement, steel, civil engineering) at a time when the domestic market had not yet fully developed.

However, the ToRE's analysis reveals above all a shift in externalities on an unprecedented scale. The dam was not built to enrich the Three Gorges region (Hubei/Chongqing), which paid an exorbitant price for the project: over a million people displaced, historic towns submerged and ecosystems destroyed. The hydroelectric power generated and the flood control benefits were harnessed for the exclusive benefit of the eastern coastal metropolises (Yangtze Delta).

The metropolitan centre (Shanghai, and beyond that the entire export-oriented coastal strip) was thus able to industrialise at breakneck speed thanks to an abundant, cheap and stable supply of electricity 'imported' from the heart of the continent. The prosperity of the metropolitan area was built upon the physical and social decline of the Middle Yangtze Valley.

Finally, the Three Gorges Dam has acted as the fundamental hydraulic hub of the Yangtze basin: without its reservoir to regulate flows across the seasons, the South-North Water Transfer Project (analysed in 4.5.3) would have been technically unfeasible. The dam thus marks the pinnacle of the CCP state's ability to reshape the country's physical geography in order to force the extraction of resources for the sake of increasing yields.

#### 4.4.2. The West-East Gas Pipeline

Whilst the Three Gorges Dam enabled water regulation, the West-East Gas Pipeline (*Xiqi Dongshu*) served a parallel and independent purpose: energy substitution. The manufacturing prosperity of the coastal regions (Shanghai, Jiangsu) required cheap energy that local coal could no longer provide without undermining profitability (pollution, logistical costs). By 'purchasing' gas from the West (Xinjiang), the CCP state has shifted the energy externality from the periphery to the centre (the Yangtze Delta).

Gas from the Taklamakan Desert constitutes a weak externality (a resource lacking access infrastructure), not a strong externality (value frozen by a symbolic prohibition). The term 'purchase' is used by analogy with the Terms of Reference, but the operation is, strictly speaking, a conventional infrastructure investment.

The account of the decision to build the West-East Gas Pipeline perfectly illustrates the theoretical sequence of the morphogenetic process specific to Structural Geography:

- **Aesthetics (Tianxia):** The geopolitical vision of the Architect of Reforms to draw a line 'with a thick, colourful brushstroke' (浓墨重彩) from west to east across the map of China, in order to open up the development of the West, is not a mere calculation of profitability, but the expression of a cosmologically inspired territorial harmony [@zhang2018bilu].

- **Policy (The Property Rule):** The Architect of Reforms — representing the CCP — takes the sovereign decision to lift the geographical ban. Downstream, Zhang Guobao, head of the National Development and Reform Commission (NDRC), acts as the technical operator of the Rent Extraction: he mobilises state credit, imposes the network in the face of local reluctance (e.g. the Rice-growing Province) and disregards the warnings of experts (CIECC), validating the political act through empirical rigour.

- **Economic (The Buyback, by analogy):** Massive financing through government debt creates 'exogenous demand in advance' (via infrastructure construction).

This case demonstrates that the Chinese model was neither a mere 'free-market miracle' nor orthodox centralised planning, but the systematic application of the laws of ToRE on a continental scale. The CCP-led state acted as the ultimate capitalist entrepreneur, 'redeeming' the geography itself to initiate a new cycle of unbalanced growth.

#### 4.4.3. The South-North Canal (South-North Water Transfer)

The prosperity of the northern metropolises (Beijing, Northern City) came up against an absolute physical limit: the depletion of groundwater reserves. Meanwhile, the Yangtze River basin, regulated by the Three Gorges Dam, was experiencing surpluses. Water, a natural element, had become an asset 'frozen' by geography: it existed but was not in the right place.

The South-North Water Diversion Project forms part of the 'Rent Extraction' mechanism. The CCP state has mobilised a colossal loan to 'buy' this distance of over 1,000 kilometres. From the perspective of the Theory of Rent Extraction (ToRE), this is the most extreme illustration of the substitution of externalities: the survival and growth of the political centre (Beijing) have been made possible by the extraction of water resources from the southern periphery. The North has bought up the South's vital resource, perpetuating the North's over-accumulation at the expense of regional balance, whilst generating the exogenous demand for the canal's construction.

#### 4.4.4. Canals providing access to the sea (e.g. the Pinglu Canal)

The second project concerns opening up mountainous hinterlands to the sea, of which the Pinglu Canal (South-West Province) is the most significant example. Historically, the south-western provinces were forced to transport their goods eastwards to reach the sea via the Pearl River Delta (Shenzhen). This dependence created an overwhelming economic advantage for the eastern coastal metropolises.

The south-western hinterland found itself in the typical position of an 'atrophied space': its productive externalities were captured by centres situated along the flow axes. To counter this centrifugal dynamic, the CCP state decided to 'redeem' the topography: by digging a canal through the mountains to link the inland rivers directly to the Gulf of Tonkin.

This project reveals a sophisticated use of the ToRE at the end of its cycle. It involves artificially injecting a maritime externality into the periphery to relaunch a micro-cycle of Rent Extraction there. By giving the South-Western Province direct access to the sea, the CCP State creates a new local land and logistics rent. The credit injected into the dredging generates exogenous demand, whilst the infrastructure is intended to attract factories, thereby recreating locally increasing returns in a region threatened by decline.

### 4.6. The exhaustion of the geographical frontier and the emergence of 'refractory values'

Far from marking the limits of the ToRE, these final projects actually confirm its predictive validity: it is the material rent extraction itself that is coming to an end. The marginal return on these operations is in free fall. The South-North Canal is accompanied by increasingly severe negative externalities (the drying up of the lower Yangtze) which are destroying the value of the donor periphery [@chen2026phosphorus]. The Pinglu Canal requires colossal investment (giant locks, tunnels) for an economic benefit that is increasingly uncertain when compared with the rail network.

The depletion of land-based finance (easily accessible urban land) has driven the CCP state to buy up the subsoil (gas), then the river (Three Gorges), then inter-basin water resources (South-North Canal), and then the topography (Pinglu). But each new acquisition required more credit to generate lower real productive returns.

Whilst some physical areas remain technically exploitable (hydroelectricity in Tibet/Qinghai, geothermal energy in Xinjiang), the costs of extraction, infrastructure and environmental externalities are such that they no longer allow for the increasing returns necessary for the cycle of buy-back. In accordance with the law of diminishing returns inherent in the Law of Returns, the marginal cost of Rent Extraction eventually exceeds the marginal value of the Wealth produced.

We propose, as a hypothesis, to extend the law of conservation of Value by classifying these territories as **refractory Values**: they exist physically, but the economic laws of the system mean that their integration into the productive cycle would require the destruction of more Wealth than it would create, thereby blocking the Rent Extraction process. This is a theoretical extension, not a concept present in the original body of work of the ToRE.

---

## Chapter 5 – The exhaustion of the centuries-long trend (Nodal Point) (2015–2018) and the sovereign management of destruction

Whilst the *Hukou* system had frozen the labour force and rural land, another stock of Value – of a different nature but subject to the same laws – had remained outside the market: disconnected territories and immobilised energy resources. As recalled in the memoirs of Zhang Guobao, the chief architect of these projects, gas from the Xinjiang desert was 'burned off into the air' (the 'lights in the sky') because there was no market to absorb it [@zhang2018bilu]. Within the framework of the ToRE, this physically isolated space (the 4,000 km between Xinjiang and Shanghai) and this non-marketable resource constituted a vast Space of Geographical Value, which subsequent mega-infrastructure projects would gradually redevelop. However, this territorial expansion, however colossal it might be, was bound to reach a limit.

### 5.1. Depletion of the stock of land and geographical value

The quantitative depletion of mobilisable assets triggers the Nodal Point theorised by the ToRE. China's growth (1978–2018) was based on the simultaneous acquisition of two stocks of value: coastal urban land and the landlocked territories of the West. Around 2015, as the urbanisation rate exceeded 65 per cent [@nbs2024realestate], this dual shift became apparent: monetised wealth exceeded the residual stock of value [@rebour2011; @guieysse2016]. Land with significant externalities around coastal metropolitan areas was fully absorbed, whilst the mega-infrastructure projects designed to open up the West reached their limits of profitability. In an attempt to prolong the cycle, local authorities then sought to buy up land in remote provinces, but these areas, lacking the necessary networks and productive advantages, attracted no solvent buyers. The saturation of the property market and the halt to mega-infrastructure projects are therefore not mere economic accidents: the cycle of Material Rent Extraction is interrupted by the simultaneous exhaustion of both the Land Value Space and the Geographical Value Space, and the Nodal Point has been reached.

This tension between the physical depletion of assets and the state's forward-looking approach had already become apparent during the design of the West-East Gas Pipeline. Experts from the International Engineering Consulting Corporation (CIECC) had assessed the proven reserves and recommended a capacity of 120 billion m³, deeming the risk associated with 200 billion to be too high. Zhang Guobao, acting as the operator of the Takeover, had opposed this, arguing that a strategic project of this scale needed to 'allow a margin' to anticipate future gas imports from Central Asia [@zhang2018bilu]. This conflict was not technical but structurally linked to the Terms of Reference (ToR): the experts reasoned in terms of 'equilibrium' (proven reserves = capacity), whilst the CCP state reasoned in terms of 'imbalance' (future reserves must be anticipated through credit to sustain exogenous demand). The fact that Zhang "gave in" to the experts (reducing the figure to 120 billion) marks an initial sign that the CCP-State's capacity to force the Buy-Back beyond physical limits is running out of steam – a prelude to the Nodal Point where the wealth created is no longer sufficient to justify the debt.

### 5.2. Morphological manifestations of the Nodal Point: ghost towns, exoregulated dispersion, reversal of hierarchies

The ToRE emphasises a corollary often overlooked by purely macroeconomic analyses: at the Nodal Point, spatial dynamics are reversed and mobility—hitherto centripetal, whether endoregulated (aggregation) or exoregulated (concentration)—gives way to centrifugal mobility: 'escape' (endoregulated) for the affluent classes, and dispersion (exoregulated) for populations forced to leave saturated centres under the pressure of costs and a shortage of affordable housing [@rebour2000, sec. 3.3]. The saturated metropolitan area drives its workforce out towards the periphery.

This is exactly what we see in China with the 'ghost towns' (such as Kangbashi in Ordos) [@wu2015ghost]. These new towns, built far from historic centres, are not merely local 'planning errors'; they are the spatial manifestation of exoregulated dispersion. Capital, no longer finding profitability in the centres (due to diminishing returns), has spread out towards undeveloped peripheries in a desperate attempt to recreate a local cycle of Rent Extraction. The reversal of urban hierarchies — with the relative decline of certain hyper-centres in favour of second- or third-tier cities for a short period — confirms that the Chinese territory has entered the morphological crisis phase of the ToRE.

The morphology of these ghost towns is no accident. The ToRE distinguishes between two main families of urban forms [@rebour2000, sections 3.1–3.3]:

- **centred forms** (circular, concentric) are associated with distributive maturity regimes and result from endoregulated mobility (chosen agglomeration);
- **eccentric forms** (sectoral, axial) are associated with regimes of polarised growth and result from exo-regulated mobility (forced concentration along export axes).

New Chinese towns such as Kangbashi, built from scratch along transport corridors and disconnected from historic urban fabric, are archetypes of the decentralised form resulting from unregulated concentration. Their failure to attract a sustainable population and economic activity is not merely a planning flaw; it is the spatial manifestation of polarised growth that has run its course. The persistence of these decentralised forms after the Nodal Point indicates that the transition to a mature, distributive regime has not taken place: the cities have not refocused on centres of domestic consumption, but have remained oriented towards transport corridors and nodes whose economic function ceased with the end of polarised growth.

This morphological observation is clarified by the regulatory role of ground rent on urban hierarchies, as analysed by the ToRE [@rebour2000, sec. 2.2]. During the upward phase of the cycle, land rent hinders the growth of cities in proportion to their size: the larger a city is, the higher the rent, and the more it discourages marginal investment, redirecting it towards lower levels of the hierarchy. This mechanism generates the rank-size harmonic function, whereby each city finds its place within a balanced hierarchy [@rebour2011a].

In China, this mechanism has been neutralised by spatial capital flows (LGFVs, state investment), which have enabled major cities to overcome the constraint of ground rent by accumulating fixed capital (skyscrapers, infrastructure). But at the Nodal Point, when these flows dry up, the regulatory logic of rent re-emerges — but in the opposite direction. Rank 3 and 4 towns, which have never benefited from sufficient capital accumulation to overcome their rent, find their rent too low to retain the capital fleeing the metropolises. They cannot absorb this dispersion, as their low rent does not generate sufficient externalities to attract investment. Morphological dispersion is thus accompanied by a frozen urban hierarchy: metropolises retain their speculative rent (even when disconnected from use value), whilst medium-sized and small towns remain structurally incapable of retaining their dynamic population.

This hierarchical deadlock has a direct impact on the metropolitan areas themselves. If third- and fourth-tier towns cannot absorb this dispersion, the capital fleeing them also fails to find productive outlets within the metropolitan areas. Speculative rent remains high there, but it is no longer supported by solvent demand. Vacant offices are proliferating in central business districts, retail outlets are closing due to a lack of customers, and retail spaces are deteriorating into urban wastelands.

This phenomenon is the physical manifestation of the disconnection between rent and use value that the ToRE identifies as a characteristic of the Nodal Point [@rebour2000, sec. 2.6]. Rent is no longer the product of productive dynamics; it is determined by speculation on a finite stock. Metropolises retain their skylines and skyscrapers, but these infrastructures, now 'ghostly', harbour empty spaces: the verticality that was once the hallmark of over-accumulation becomes the symbol of its exhaustion.

### 5.3. The sovereign destruction of parasitic wealth: the 'Three Red Lines' and the Evergrande precedent

The law of conservation of ToRE stipulates that, at the Nodal Point, the destruction of Wealth is necessary to recreate the conditions for a new cycle. In Western economies, this destruction takes place chaotically through the market: a cascade of bankruptcies, collapsing prices, mass unemployment, and the socialisation of banking losses (the 2008 scenario). The CCP-led state, drawing on its capacity for strategic coordination, has chosen a radically different path: selective sovereign demolition.

The 'Three Red Lines' policy (三条红线), launched in August 2020, illustrates how the CCP-led state has taken control of managing over-accumulation, refusing to delegate it solely to market forces. By imposing strict debt ratios on property developers, the CCP-State has orchestrated a targeted reduction in the sector's overcapacity.

The case of Evergrande illustrates the logic behind this structural selection. The group had taken on massive debt on Hong Kong's offshore markets, issuing dollar-denominated bonds and thereby integrating the Chinese property sector into the sphere of international financialisation. This connection served as a conduit for extraterritorial risks: should the economic cycle reverse, foreign creditors could have exerted destabilising pressure on the domestic financial system. By allowing Evergrande to default on its offshore debt whilst prosecuting its executives for embezzlement, the CCP-led state has effected a threefold structural adjustment:

- **Punishment for rent-seeking behaviour:** the destruction of the developer's shareholder and financial wealth, reaffirming that virtuous legitimacy (*De*) takes precedence over speculative private profit;
- **Monetary and geopolitical firewall:** cutting off the channel of contagion from Western financial speculation, protecting the domestic banking system from the extraterritorial reach of the dollar and shielding the Chinese economic cycle from the Federal Reserve's interest rate cycles;
- **Preservation of the social contract:** through the slogan *Baojiaolou* (保交楼 — 'Guarantee the delivery of housing'), the CCP state has split Wealth into two components. It has sacrificed financial Wealth (offshore creditors, shareholders, executives), but has mobilised public resources to complete the construction of housing pre-sold to households. Usufructual Wealth — the family's concrete territorial anchoring in their home — was protected in the name of virtuous legitimacy (*De*), whilst speculative Wealth was left to be destroyed.

This sequence can be interpreted as an illustration of the logic of the ToRE in its political dimension: the destruction of wealth at the Nodal Point is mandatory within the theoretical framework, but the manner in which it occurs — whether chaotically through the market or sovereignly through the CCP-led state — depends on institutional and political structures. Whereas the West socialised the banks' losses to save the financial system in 2008, China took the opposite approach, sacrificing property developers to save households and preserve the social contract. This difference does not validate the theory, but illustrates one possible application within a specific institutional context.

However, the Theory of the Nodal Point teaches that the destruction of wealth is only the first half of the Nodal Point equation. For the cycle to restart, this destruction must free up capital for a new Rent Extraction, a new Space of Value capable of generating exogenous demand in advance. Yet, by dismantling the model of off-plan property sales — which relied on households' confidence in future delivery — the CCP state has simultaneously dried up the historical mechanism of local money creation. Selection has eliminated the parasite, but the patient finds itself paralysed. Local governments, already suffocated by the LGFVs, are faced with a void in exogenous demand that neither conventional bank credit nor central budgetary transfers can fill. It is this structural impasse, and not a misdiagnosis, that makes the search for a new frontier imperative.

### 5.4. The closure of physical internal borders and the need for a Third Transformation

Faced with this impasse, the CCP State is confronted with the theoretical alternative posed by the Nodal Point: to allow the destruction of Wealth to proceed unchecked (the Western scenario), or to find a new frontier capable of recreating mobilisable Value. The CCP State's prudent judgement, as embodied in the Three Red Lines, has addressed the first half of the equation; what remains is to identify the new stock of Value.

Any analysis of Chinese overseas investment must distinguish between two structurally different categories. The transport corridors of the Silk Roads — gas pipelines, oil pipelines, railways and maritime routes — are transit infrastructures that secure physical flows between China and global markets. Their profitability does not depend on local sovereignty: the West-East Gas Pipeline, the Central Asia–Europe railways and the Russia–Empire pipelines remain operational because they are underpinned by guaranteed flows and strategic corridors.

By contrast, local productive investments — HPAL factories in Indonesia, the port of Chancay (Peru), the port of Hambantota (Sri Lanka) and the port of Piraeus (Greece) — have had mixed results. The ports, designed as '**Fortified Port Enclaves**' (§5.5.5), have been successful: they benefit from exclusive rights, regulatory exemptions and full collateral, which make any expropriation economically devastating for the host country. Processing plants, on the other hand, are structurally vulnerable to **Sovereign Rent**^['Sovereign Rent' refers to the economic value attached to a state's ability to unilaterally alter the rules of the game within its territory (quotas, exchange rates, taxation). This is triggered when the host state alters the operating conditions for a foreign asset. Fortified Port Enclaves are designed to shield investments against this rent.] of the host state: Indonesian Decree No. 144 (2026) demonstrated that a sovereign state can unilaterally alter the rules of the game (quotas, costs, currencies), rendering the Chinese financial arrangement untenable [@esdm2026decree144; @scmp2026indonesia].

The lesson of the Third Transformation is that China now favours logistics enclaves (ports, pipelines) over processing plants, as the former are immune to the host states' Sovereignty Rent. The external material frontier is therefore virtually closed for industrial processing, but remains open for logistics enclaves — a distinction that Chinese strategy has incorporated. Whilst the infrastructure of the Silk Roads has partially succeeded in organising the spatial flows of the *Tianxia* [@cai2021bri], it has not generated the 'advance exogenous demand' necessary to absorb the shock of the domestic Nodal Point. The economic returns on external investment in transformation are proving too low and too slow to compensate for the abrupt halt to the internal driving force [@hurley2019docking]. With the external material frontier virtually closed to industry, the systemic constraint is tightening.

It is against this backdrop that the strategic planning capacity of the unified CCP-State is confronted with its deepest epistemological limit. The epistemology of the infinite development of the productive forces comes up against the law of conservation of ToRE: the stock of Value is finite at any given point in time, technology and geography. The capacity for inter-regional coordination that has enabled the construction of ultra-high-voltage networks, high-speed railways and inter-basin water transfers cannot, on its own, create a new stock of Value where physical geography no longer affords it. The CCP State can coordinate, plan and allocate; it cannot decree the existence of a virgin 'Space of Value' once the territory has been fully appropriated.

To uphold the Mandate of Heaven and preserve the social harmony underpinning the '*De*', the CCP State is therefore compelled to effect a radical internal shift. The '**Third Transformation**' towards the immaterial (carbon, biological data, sovereign artificial intelligence) is neither a headlong rush nor a denial of reality: it constitutes the most ambitious sovereign attempt to transcend the law of conservation through the creation of new normative scarcities, in order to avoid the social cost of widespread material destruction, which the implicit contract between the CCP-State and the population renders politically unacceptable. It is this systemic constraint — the need to recreate a cycle of Rent Extraction without having a new geographical substrate — that justifies the launch of this transformation and which, at the same time, constitutes its structural limit, as will be demonstrated in Part III.

### 5.5. The externalisation of the destruction of wealth and the structural dependence of the periphery

The Third Transformation, analysed in its immaterial dimension, raises an operational question: how does China secure its supply of material substrates (minerals, energy, components) whilst its domestic economy has entered a state of Stasis? The answer lies in a transformation of the role of the periphery within the global economic system.

#### 5.5.1. The geographical externalisation of the destruction of Wealth

The ToRE teaches that at the Nodal Point, material wealth must be destroyed so that rent can be recreated elsewhere (see §1.7). In the historical Western model, this destruction affected both the centre and the periphery indiscriminately. China, by retreating into the immaterial, has altered this situation.

The Chinese strategy consists of relocating part of the destruction of wealth to the host states of its foreign investments. This mechanism is not a malicious intention, but a structural consequence of the asymmetry between Chinese assets (intangible, immune) and assets located abroad (physical, vulnerable). China can repatriate its strategic equipment and technologies, leaving the host country with unfinished or inoperative infrastructure. The depreciation of assets is thus geographically localised within the host country's territory, not that of the centre.

This transfer is not a deliberate strategy but a structural feature of the system: Chinese intangible assets (tokens, quotas, exploitation rights) are immune to physical shocks, whilst tangible assets located abroad are exposed to them. This structural asymmetry makes the periphery more vulnerable to cycles of devaluation than the centre.

#### 5.5.2. The costs and limitations of withdrawal

It would, however, be mistaken to think that China emerges unscathed from these withdrawals. The dismantling of the HPAL plants in Sulawesi in 2026 resulted in real losses: loans from the CDB and the Export Bank remain largely unrepaid, future cash flows have been lost, and China's image as a reliable partner has been tarnished. China has certainly repatriated its titanium autoclaves and its know-how, but it has lost several billion dollars in investment and a strategic position in the nickel supply chain. Indonesia, for its part, has lost factories that it cannot operate without Chinese reagents and engineering expertise, but it has preserved its sovereignty over its resources — a significant political gain.

The case of Venezuela offers an instructive parallel. Beijing had granted massive loans to Caracas (over $50 billion between 2007 and 2015), secured against oil deliveries. The collapse of the Venezuelan economy and political instability led to a renegotiation of the debt: China had to accept losses, rescheduling and a reduction in its claims [@hurley2019docking]. The host state's Sovereign Annuity was triggered, not by a decree, but by institutional collapse, which made debt servicing impossible. China was unable to seize Venezuelan oil assets without risking permanently jeopardising its relationship with a strategic partner. It therefore had to absorb part of the destruction of wealth.

The difference with Indonesia lies in the nature of the asset: the HPAL plants were localised assets, which were partially seizable; Venezuelan oil is an underground asset, less easily transferable. But in both cases, China suffered losses. The asymmetry is therefore not absolute: it is a structural bias which, on average, protects the centre, but does not provide total immunity.

#### 5.5.3. Debt as an instrument of structural dependence

China finances its overseas investments through loans from the CDB and the China Exim Bank, secured against the future cash flows of the projects (see §4.3). This arrangement, similar to the Land Finance model, works as long as the host state does not exercise its sovereign rent — that is, its ability to unilaterally alter the rules of the game (quotas, exchange rates, taxation). When Jakarta activated this rent via Decree No. 144, the financial arrangement collapsed. China was forced to withdraw, leaving Indonesia with unfinished infrastructure and unpaid debts, whilst bearing part of the cost itself.

Beijing has learnt its lesson: future investments must be designed to be more resilient to the host's Sovereignty Rent. This is the rationale behind **Fortified Port Enclaves** (see §5.5.5) — de facto extraterritorial infrastructure, protected by exclusive rights, regulatory exemptions and full collateral, which make any unilateral change to the rules economically destructive for the host country. This lesson was drawn in part from the Indonesian and Venezuelan withdrawals: where the host can change the rules, China must secure its positions through legal and financial architecture, not through political trust.

China's external debt is therefore not an instrument of a 'debt trap' but a mechanism of structural dependence. It creates an asymmetry in which the host state bears the bulk of the risks of devaluation, whilst the Chinese centre retains control over the normative assets that guarantee the debt. This asymmetry, however, has its limits: if losses mount up (as in Venezuela), China is forced to absorb them, which reduces the scope of externalisation.

#### 5.5.4. Agricultural outsourcing: leased land in Russia and global agro-industrial partnerships

Since the 2010s, China has pursued a strategy of outsourcing agricultural production to vast tracts of underutilised land abroad. This strategy, which forms part of an approach to securing food supplies, takes various forms depending on the region and institutional context.

**The Russian case: the long-term lease model**

The area of land leased or controlled by Chinese interests in Russia exceeds 670,000 hectares (according to SIPRI estimates), and could reach over one million hectares according to more recent sources, including 426,600 hectares leased directly near the border. The terms of these leases are clearly defined: 49-year contracts, an annual rent of 300 to 600 yuan per hectare, and an obligation to employ at least 30 per cent Russian labour. The produce (soya, wheat, maize, meat) is partly exported to China, contributing to the country's food security, as it is only 50 per cent self-sufficient in soya.

**Other agricultural fronts: Africa, Latin America and South-East Asia**

Beyond Russia, China has rolled out a global agricultural investment strategy, often referred to as 'going out' in the agri-food sector. The main areas of investment include:

- **Sub-Saharan Africa:** China is active in several countries, employing a variety of approaches. In Madagascar, cooperation focuses mainly on technology transfer (hybrid rice) and public-private partnerships, without large-scale land leasing. In Angola, a 130,000-hectare project is dedicated to the cultivation of soya and maize, with 60 per cent of the production destined for export to China. In Tanzania, Chinese companies have developed projects covering 32,000 hectares. In Zambia, investments have been made on land leased on 99-year leases. In the Democratic Republic of the Congo, a 2.8 million-hectare project is planned for a palm oil plantation. Other projects exist in Senegal, Sierra Leone and Mozambique.

- **Latin America:** China has invested in agricultural land in Brazil, Argentina and Uruguay, mainly for the production of soya, maize and cereals. These investments are often made through trade agreements or stakes in local companies, rather than through direct leases.

- **South-East Asia:** Investments have been made in Laos, Cambodia, Thailand and Myanmar, where Chinese companies grow rice, rubber and energy crops (sugar cane, palm oil).

**ToRE typology of Chinese agricultural partnerships abroad**

Within the framework of the Rent Extraction Theory, these operations differ radically from the Chinese land tenure model. This case illustrates a weak externality (an under-utilised resource lacking access infrastructure or market access), not a Rent Extraction in the strict sense of the ToRE. Unlike Chinese land, foreign land is not 'frozen' by an anthropological or symbolic taboo. It is simply under-utilised and subject to sovereign regulation. China leases, it does not buy; it produces, it does not speculate.
